(5.27)

Substituting this ansatz into the recursion relation in (
5.25
), and evaluating the integral in the last term on the right-hand side, yields a linear equation that can be solved for
A

\displaystyle A=\frac{\sqrt{\lambda_{0}}(N+8)\Gamma\!\left(\frac{1}{4}\right)}{2^{\frac{19}{4}}\sqrt{3}\,\pi^{\frac{5}{2}}}\bigg(\frac{1}{\varepsilon}\,+

(5.28)

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad+\frac{32-2\pi+3\gamma_{E}+13\log(2)-3\sqrt{2}\log\left(2\sqrt{2}+3\right)}{4}+\text{O}(\varepsilon)\bigg)\,.

As we argued, (
5.28
) captures the full divergence of the more complicated diagram in (
5.22
). A non-trivial consistency check is that this divergence exactly cancels the one arising from the coupling renormalization in the classical term. More specifically, we have

\langle\phi_{a}(x)\rangle=\phi^{a}_{\text{cl}}+\langle\delta\phi_{a}(x)\rangle\,,

(5.29)

where
\phi^{a}_{\text{cl}}\sim 1/\sqrt{\lambda_{0}}
as given by (
5.17
). Since the bulk renormalizes independently, we also have that
\lambda_{0}=\mu^{\varepsilon}\lambda(1+\lambda(N+8)/(12\pi^{2}\varepsilon)+\text{O}(\lambda^{2}))
, according to (
2.11
).
Therefore, the first term in (
5.22
) has a divergence at order
\sqrt{\lambda}
, which is exactly canceled by the divergence in the second term that we just computed. This ensures that
\langle\phi_{a}(x)\rangle
remains finite.
The finite term corresponds to the order
\varepsilon^{0}
correction to (
5.19
). Part of this finite term is given in (
5.28
), while the rest comes from diagrams that contain more than one quadratic interaction in the loop. Their contributions can be collected into one single diagram

(5.30)

5.25
), it suffices to compute the following

(5.31)

Unfortunately, performing this computation analytically is difficult. However, all the diagrams in the sum in (
5.31
) are finite for
\varepsilon=0
and can be evaluated numerically. The result of the sum can then be extrapolated using standard numerical techniques, such as Padé approximation. We leave this analysis for future investigations.

\displaystyle=4\left(\mathcal{N}_{\phi}^{2}\right)^{3}Nh_{0}^{2}\tfrac{\pi^{3}\Gamma\left(2\Delta_{\phi}-1\right)^{2}\Gamma\left(3\Delta_{\phi}-2\right)}{\Gamma(\Delta_{\phi})^{3}\Gamma\left(4\Delta_{\phi}-2\right)\sin(\pi\Delta_{\phi})}\tfrac{1}{|x_{\perp}|^{6\Delta_{\phi}-4}}\,,

(A.7)

(A.8)

Appendix B
Useful integrals

The diagrams above make use of the following integrals.

Integral over a bulk vertex

I(x):=\int\frac{d^{d}y}{|x-y|^{A}|y|^{B}}=\frac{w_{A}^{(d)}w_{B}^{(d)}}{w_{A+B-d}^{(d)}}\frac{1}{|x|^{A+B-d}}

(B.1)

Integral over a defect vertex

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{p}\tau}{|x-x(\tau)|^{\alpha}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{\frac{p}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha-p}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\frac{1}{|x_{\perp}|^{\alpha-p}}\,.

(B.2)

Integral over a bulk vertex with defect propagator

\displaystyle\int\frac{d^{d}y}{\left|x-y\right|^{\alpha}\left|y_{\|}\right|^{\beta}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{\frac{d-p}{2}}\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha-d+p}{2}\right)}{\Gamma\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}\frac{w_{\alpha-d+p}^{(p)}w_{\beta}^{(p)}}{w_{\alpha+\beta-d}^{(p)}}\frac{1}{\left|x_{\|}\right|^{\alpha+\beta-d}}\,.

(B.3)

Three propagators

The following integral is used in the renormalization of the surface defect coupling, and is particularly challenging to compute by hand (borrowing notation from
[
56
]
, see
[
82
]
for a derivation). One way to proceed is to use Schwinger parametrization and the inversion formula for the gamma function

\displaystyle F

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{d}kd^{d}l}{\left(k^{2}+m^{2}\right)^{\lambda_{1}}\left[\left(k+l\right)^{2}\right]^{\lambda_{2}}\left(l^{2}+m^{2}\right)^{\lambda_{3}}}

(B.4)

\displaystyle=\frac{\pi^{d}\Gamma\left(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}-\frac{d}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-\frac{d}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}-\lambda_{2}\right)\Gamma\left(\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-d\right)}{\Gamma\left(\lambda_{1}\right)\Gamma\left(\lambda_{3}\right)\Gamma\left(\lambda_{1}+2\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-d\right)\Gamma\left(\frac{d}{2}\right)}\frac{1}{\left(m^{2}\right)^{\lambda_{1}+\lambda_{2}+\lambda_{3}-d}}\,.

Appendix C
Diagrams for the
g
-function close to four dimensions

First, we list the diagrams useful to the computation of the
g
-function close to four dimensions for the localized magnetic field. The second diagram is challenging to compute exactly, and the result up to
\mathcal{O}(\hat{\varepsilon})
given below makes use of the integral (
C.9
).

\displaystyle=-\lambda_{0}h_{0}^{2}\left(\mathcal{N}_{\phi}^{2}\right)^{4}\frac{N(N+2)}{6}\frac{\left[w_{4\Delta_{\phi}}^{(d)}\right]^{2}}{w_{8\Delta_{\phi}-d}^{(d)}}\frac{(2R)^{d+4-8\Delta_{\phi}}\pi^{2}}{\frac{d}{2}+1-4\Delta_{\phi}}\,.

(C.5)

C.1
Useful integrals for the computation of
g
-functions

The diagrams above make use of the following integrals.

Circular integral over one angle

\displaystyle\oint\frac{d\tau}{|x-x(\tau)|^{2\Delta}}

\displaystyle=\int_{-\pi}^{\pi}\frac{Rd\theta}{\left(\left|x_{\|}\right|^{2}+\left|x_{\perp}\right|^{2}+R^{2}-2R\left|x_{\|}\right|\cos{\theta}\right)^{\Delta}}

(C.6)

\displaystyle=\frac{2\pi R}{\left[\left(\left|x_{\|}\right|+R\right)^{2}+\left|x_{\perp}\right|^{2}\right]^{\Delta}}{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\frac{1}{2},\Delta;1,\frac{4R\left|x_{\|}\right|}{\left(\left|x_{\|}\right|+R\right)^{2}+\left|x_{\perp}\right|^{2}}\right)\,,

where we’ve used the integral representation of the hypergeometric function
{}_{2}F_{1}
.

Circular integral over two angles

\displaystyle\oint\frac{d\tau_{1}d\tau_{2}}{|x(\tau_{1})-x(\tau_{2})|^{2\Delta}}

\displaystyle=4^{1-\Delta}R^{2-2\Delta}\pi\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}-\Delta\right)\sqrt{\pi}}{\Gamma\left(1-\Delta\right)}\,.

(C.7)

Second diagram in the
g
-function for the localized magnetic field

The computation of diagram (
C.2
) is intricate. Calculating it amounts to evaluating the following quantity

\displaystyle-\frac{\lambda_{0}h_{0}^{4}}{4!}\left(\mathcal{N}_{\phi}^{2}\right)^{4}\int d^{d}x\left(\oint\frac{d\tau}{|x-x(\tau)|^{2\Delta_{\phi}}}\right)^{4}=-\frac{\lambda_{0}h_{0}^{4}}{4!}I\,.

(C.8)

The integral over the circular defect can be computed using equation (
C.6
). It simplifies to

\displaystyle I=2\pi\left(2\pi R\right)^{4}\left(\mathcal{N}_{\phi}^{2}\right)^{4}S_{2-\varepsilon}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!dr\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\!dz\tfrac{rz^{3-\varepsilon}}{\left(\left(r+R\right)^{2}+z^{2}\right)^{4-(1+\kappa)\varepsilon}}\left[{}_{2}F_{1}\left(\tfrac{1}{2},1-\tfrac{(1+\kappa)\varepsilon}{4};1,\tfrac{4rR}{\left(r+R\right)^{2}+z^{2}}\right)\right]^{4}\,.

(C.9)

It is enough to give
I
to order
\text{O}\left(\hat{\varepsilon}^{0}\right)
, since
\lambda_{*}\sim\hat{\varepsilon}
and
h_{*}
is finite. The remaining integral can be conducted using appendix B in
[
42
]
and it evaluates to
I=1/(16\pi^{2})
in the limite
\hat{\varepsilon}\to 0
.

Spherical integration over two angles

\displaystyle\oint\frac{d^{2}\tau_{1}d^{2}\tau_{2}}{|x(\tau_{1})-x(\tau_{2})|^{2\Delta}}=\frac{(2R)^{4-2\Delta}\pi^{2}}{1-\Delta}\,.

(C.10)

Appendix D
Integrating out
\hat{\psi}

Let’s study the effective theory for
\phi
when
\hat{\psi}
is integrated out in a theory described by the action (
2.16
). The effective action thus obtained yields results identical to those derived in the two-field picture, but it is nevertheless instructive to show that the relevant functional integrals can be conducted in some simple cases. Furthermore, having noticed that in
d=4
the dimension
\Delta_{\psi}\to 0
for
a=p
, one might want to check whether or not the aforementioned action reduces to that of a localized magnetic field for
a=p=1
(resp. surface defect for
a=p=2
) as
d\to 4
.

Throughout this section, we’ll be considering a free bulk, i.e.
\lambda\to 0
, and we will argue that in some cases where the defect GFF can be integrated out, there is no match with the localized magnetic field and the surface defect when
d=4-\hat{\varepsilon}
.

Let’s set
\sigma=d-2\Delta_{\phi}
and
\tau=p-2\Delta_{\psi}
. To avoid conflicts and use lighter notation, we also drop the
\tau
parametrization of the defect and instead use
y
coordinates in this section only. It turns out that for
b=1
or
b=2
– which are the only cases we’ll look at – this integration can be carried out exactly. This amounts to the following rewriting of the partition function

Z=\int\mathcal{D}\phi e^{-S_{0}[\phi]}\int\mathcal{D}\psi e^{-S_{1}[\phi,\psi]}=\int\mathcal{D}\phi e^{-S_{\text{eff}}[\phi]}\,,

(D.1)

where
S_{0}[\phi]
is the bulk action,
S_{1}[\phi,\psi]
is the defect action and the effective action is defined, up to a
\phi
-independent term, by

S_{\text{eff}}[\phi]:=S_{0}[\phi]-\log\int\mathcal{D}\psi e^{-S_{1}[\phi,\psi]}\,.

(D.2)

D.1
b=1
theory

To integrate out
\psi
in the
b=1
case, one can start by completing the square in the defect action

S_{1}[\phi,\psi]=\frac{1}{2}\int_{p}\left[\psi\mathcal{L}_{\tau}\psi+g_{0}\phi^{a}\psi\right]=\frac{1}{2}\int_{p}\left(\psi+\frac{g_{0}\phi^{a}\mathcal{L}_{-\tau}}{4}\right)\mathcal{L}_{\tau}\left(\psi+\frac{g_{0}\mathcal{L}_{-\tau}\phi^{a}}{4}\right)-\frac{g_{0}^{2}}{16}\int_{p}\phi^{a}\mathcal{L}_{-\tau}\phi^{a}\,.

(D.3)

A simple translation of
\psi
makes the first term in
S_{1}

\phi
-independent, and the resulting path integral is a constant. We are hence left with

\displaystyle S_{\text{eff}}[\phi]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{d}x\phi\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\phi-\frac{g_{0}^{2}}{16}\int d^{p}y\phi^{a}\mathcal{L}_{-\tau}\phi^{a}

(D.4)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{d}x\phi\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\phi-\frac{g_{0}^{2}}{16}\mathcal{N}_{-\tau}\int d^{p}y_{1}d^{p}y_{2}\frac{\phi^{a}(y_{1})\phi^{a}(y_{2})}{|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{p-\tau}}\,.

Taking
d\to 4
in the
a=p
case yields to lowest nontrivial order
13
13

13

We ignore the normalization factor in front of
g_{0}
, since the coupling can be redefined to absorb it as we attempt to map to the localized magnetic field or the surface defect.

\displaystyle S_{\text{eff}}[\phi]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{d}x\phi\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\phi-\left(g_{0}\int d^{p}y\phi^{p}(y)\right)^{2}\,,

(D.5)

since
\Delta_{\psi}\to 0
. This action clearly can’t be mapped to the localized magnetic field, nor to the surface defect by a simple redefinition of
g_{0}
.

D.2
b=2
theory

In the
b=2
case, integrating out
\psi
amounts to evaluating a Gaussian integral.

S_{1}[\phi]=\frac{1}{2}\int dx\psi\left(\mathcal{L}_{\tau}+g_{0}\phi^{a}\right)\psi\,.

(D.6)

This looks like a GFF,
g\phi^{a}
generating a mass-like term for
\psi
. We need to calculate the following functional integral to integrate this field out

\int\mathcal{D}\psi e^{-S_{1}}=C\det\left(\mathcal{L}_{\tau}+g_{0}\phi^{a}\right)^{-1/2}=Ce^{-\int d^{p}xL_{\text{eff}}}\,,

(D.7)

where
C
is some normalization constant that isn’t important and we introduce an effective Lagrangian. Computing the effective action amounts to using the following identity for an operator
\hat{A}

\log\det\hat{A}=\textup{Tr}\log\hat{A}\,.

(D.8)

Hence the difficult task of calculating a determinant can be recast into a trace calculation. The following equalities hold modulo a
\phi
-independent term

\displaystyle\textup{Tr}\log\left(\mathcal{L}_{\tau}+g\phi^{a}\right)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{p}k}{(2\pi)^{p}}\left\langle k\left|\log\left(\mathcal{L}_{\tau}+g_{0}\phi^{a}\right)\right|k\right\rangle

(D.9)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{p}k}{(2\pi)^{p}}d^{p}xe^{ikx}\left\langle x\left|\log\left(\mathcal{L}_{\tau}+g_{0}\phi^{a}\right)\right|k\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\int\frac{d^{p}k}{(2\pi)^{p}}d^{p}xe^{ikx}\left\langle x\left|\log\left(1+g_{0}\mathcal{L}_{\tau}^{-1}\phi^{a}\right)\right|k\right\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{+\infty}\frac{g_{0}^{j}(-1)^{j+1}}{j}\int d^{p}x\frac{d^{p}k}{(2\pi)^{p}}e^{ikx}\left\langle x\left|\left[\mathcal{L}_{\tau}^{-1}\phi^{a}\right]^{j}\right|k\right\rangle\,.

One can then use the following identity, easily derived using the action of the fractional Laplacian on plane waves.

\left\langle x\left|\left[\mathcal{L}_{\tau}^{-1}\phi^{a}\right]^{j}\right|k\right\rangle=e^{-ikx}\int\prod_{i=1}^{j}\frac{d^{p}q_{i}}{(2\pi)^{p}}\hat{\phi^{a}}(q_{i})e^{iq_{i}x}\frac{1}{\left|q_{1}+\dots+q_{i}-k\right|^{\tau}}\,.

(D.10)

We finally get
L_{\text{eff}}=\sum_{j}L_{\text{eff}}^{(j)}
with

\displaystyle L_{\text{eff}}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{0}^{j}(-1)^{j+1}}{2j}\int\frac{d^{p}k}{(2\pi)^{p}}\prod_{i=1}^{j}\frac{d^{p}q_{i}}{(2\pi)^{p}}\hat{\phi^{a}}(q_{i})e^{iq_{i}x}\frac{1}{\left|q_{1}+\dots+q_{i}-k\right|^{\tau}}

(D.11)

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{0}^{j}(-1)^{j+1}}{2j}\phi(x)^{a}\int\frac{d^{p}k}{(2\pi)^{p}}\frac{1}{|k|^{\tau}}\prod_{i=2}^{j}\frac{d^{p}q_{i}}{(2\pi)^{p}}\hat{\phi^{a}}(q_{i})e^{iq_{i}x}\frac{1}{\left|q_{2}+\dots+q_{i}-k\right|^{\tau}}

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{0}^{j}(-1)^{j+1}}{2j}\phi(x)^{a}\int\frac{d^{p}k}{(2\pi)^{p}}\frac{1}{|k|^{\tau}}\prod_{i=2}^{j}\frac{d^{p}q_{i}}{(2\pi)^{p}}d^{p}x_{i}\phi(x_{i})^{a}e^{iq_{i}(x-x_{i})}\frac{1}{\left|q_{2}+\dots+q_{i}-k\right|^{\tau}}\,.

Reorganizing the complex exponentials allows one to rewrite this using known propagators, yielding

S_{\text{eff}}=\sum_{j=1}^{+\infty}\frac{(-1)^{j+1}\left(\mathcal{N}_{\psi}^{2}\right)^{j}}{2j}g_{0}^{j}\int\prod_{i=1}^{j}d^{p}x_{i}\frac{\phi(x_{i})^{a}}{|x_{i+1}-x_{i}|^{p-\tau}}\,.

(D.12)

Setting
a=p=1
or
2
and taking
d\to 4
, the effective action becomes, to lowest nontrivial order

S_{\text{eff}}[\phi]=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{d}x\phi\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\phi+\frac{1}{2}\log\left(1+g_{0}\int d^{p}y\phi(y)^{p}\right)\,.

(D.13)

This action seems a lot closer to the localized magnetic field and the surface defect. As we have seen,
g
renormalizes in a few of the interesting cases, and the fixed point is infinitesimal. Hence, expanding the logarithm leads to

S_{\text{eff}}=\frac{1}{2}\int d^{d}x\phi\mathcal{L}_{\sigma}\phi+\frac{g_{0}}{2}\int d^{p}y\phi(y)^{p}+\dots

(D.14)

Therefore, at the lowest orders, the theory does resemble the line or surface defects we studied. Notwithstanding, their behaviors diverge from one another as we include higher loop orders. One observable which exemplifies this is the
g
-function for the
a=p=1
theory, which is readily accessible from the effective theory

\displaystyle g=\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}\frac{\left(4k\right)!}{\left(2k\right)!k!}\left[g_{0}^{2}2^{-3-2\Delta_{\phi}}R^{2-2\Delta_{\phi}}\pi\frac{\Gamma\left(\frac{1}{2}-\Delta_{\phi}\right)\sqrt{\pi}}{\Gamma\left(1-\Delta_{\phi}\right)}\right]^{k}\,.

(D.15)

The above expression cannot directly be mapped to (
4.18
) by a redefinition of
g_{0}
. This is essentially because of the coefficients
(4k)!/(2k)!
making it depart from the series of an exponential. Incidentally, these coefficients also translate the different combinatorics in the two theories on the line. Indeed, when
\Delta_{\psi}\to 0
, some diagrams which were previously distinct become the same, and contribute non-trivially to the combinatorics.
